=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple undirected graph with N vertices and M edges. The i-th edge connects vertices u_i and v_i bidirectionally.
Determine if there exists a way to write an integer between 1 and 2^{60} - 1, inclusive, on each vertex of this graph so that the following condition is satisfied:

- For every vertex v with a degree of at least 1, the total XOR of the numbers written on its adjacent vertices (excluding v itself) is 0.


What is XOR?

The XOR of two non-negative integers A and B, denoted as A \oplus B, is defined as follows:


- In the binary representation of A \oplus B, the bit at position 2^k \, (k \geq 0) is 1 if and only if exactly one of the bits at position 2^k in the binary representations of A and B is 1. Otherwise, it is 0.


For example, 3 \oplus 5 = 6 (in binary: 011 \oplus 101 = 110).

In general, the bitwise XOR of k integers p_1, \dots, p_k is defined as (\cdots ((p_1 \oplus p_2) \oplus p_3) \oplus \cdots \oplus p_k).  It can be proved that this is independent of the order of p_1, \dots, p_k.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
u_1 v_1
u_2 v_2
\vdots
u_M v_M

Output

If there is no way to write integers satisfying the condition, print No.
Otherwise, let X_v be the integer written on vertex v, and print your solution in the following format. If multiple solutions exist, any of them will be accepted.
Yes
X_1 X_2 \dots X_N

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 60
- 0 \leq M \leq N(N-1)/2
- 1 \leq u_i < v_i \leq N
- (u_i, v_i) \neq (u_j, v_j) for i \neq j.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 3
1 2
1 3
2 3

Sample Output 1

Yes
4 4 4

Other acceptable solutions include writing (2,2,2) or (3,3,3).

Sample Input 2

2 1
1 2

Sample Output 2

No

Sample Input 3

1 0

Sample Output 3

Yes
1

Any integer between 1 and 2^{60} - 1 can be written.

Sample Input 4

4 5
1 2
1 3
2 3
2 4
3 4

Sample Output 4

Yes
12 4 4 8


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Need ensure Gaussian elimination with int bitmasks and RREF works for N=60. Let's design code.

Pseudo:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N,M = map(int,input().split())
    A = [0]*N
    for _ in range(M):
        u,v = map(int,input().split())
        u-=1; v-=1
        A[u] ^= 1<<v
        A[v] ^= 1<<u
    rows = A[:]  # mutable
    pivot_row_for_col = [-1]*N
    pivot_cols = []
    rank = 0
    for col in range(N):
        # find pivot row
        pivot = -1
        for r in range(rank, N):
            if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:
                pivot = r
                break
        if pivot == -1:
            continue
        # swap
        rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]
        # eliminate this column from all other rows
        for r in range(N):
            if r != rank and ((rows[r] >> col) & 1):
                rows[r] ^= rows[rank]
        pivot_row_for_col[col] = rank
        pivot_cols.append(col)
        rank += 1
    # free columns are those with pivot_row_for_col[col] == -1
    free_cols = [c for c in range(N) if pivot_row_for_col[c] == -1]
    basis = []
    for f in free_cols:
        vec = 1 << f
        for p in pivot_cols:
            r = pivot_row_for_col[p]
            if (rows[r] >> f) & 1:
                vec |= 1 << p
        basis.append(vec)
    # coverage
    covered = [False]*N
    for vec in basis:
        # iterate set bits
        x = vec
        while x:
            lsb = x & -x
            v = lsb.bit_length()-1
            covered[v] = True
            x -= lsb
    if not all(covered):
        print("No")
        return
    # construct X
    X = [0]*N
    for j, vec in enumerate(basis):
        bit = 1 << j
        # for each vertex v with bit set in vec, add bit
        x = vec
        while x:
            lsb = x & -x
            v = lsb.bit_length()-1
            X[v] |= bit
            x -= lsb
    print("Yes")
    print(" ".join(map(str,X)))

Let's examine the Gaussian elimination code in detail for correctness with RREF and basis.

```
rows = A[:]
pivot_row_for_col = [-1]*N
pivot_cols = []
rank = 0
for col in range(N):
    pivot = -1
    for r in range(rank, N):
        if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:
            pivot = r
            break
    if pivot == -1:
        continue
    rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]
    for r in range(N):
        if r != rank and ((rows[r] >> col) & 1):
            rows[r] ^= rows[rank]
    pivot_row_for_col[col] = rank
    pivot_cols.append(col)
    rank += 1
```

Suppose pivot found at r > rank. We swap. Then eliminate col from all rows except rank. But what about the row that was at rank before swap? It is now at pivot position. It may have bit col set? The pivot row (now at rank) has bit col set. We eliminate from all other rows, including the old rank row now at pivot? Wait after swap, rows[rank] is pivot row. The row originally at rank is at pivot (r). It is "other row" and may have bit col set? Let's see: We searched for pivot among rows rank..N-1. The row at rank might or might not have bit col set. If it did, pivot would be rank. If not, pivot > rank. After swap, the old row at rank moves to pivot position. It did NOT have bit col set (since pivot search found first with bit col set; if row rank didn't have it, pivot > rank). So eliminating col from all rows except rank includes old row at pivot position; it has bit col=0, so no XOR. Other rows below may have bit col set. Good. If pivot==rank, old row is pivot, no issue.

But what about rows above rank? They are previous pivot rows. They may have bit col set. We eliminate by XORing pivot row. Pivot row has zeros in previous pivot columns, so previous pivot columns remain. Good.

Now, after eliminating col from all rows, the pivot row may have bits in columns < col that are free (skipped). It may also have bits in columns > col. It has zeros in previous pivot columns. Good.

When we later process a later column d, we search from rank (which is after current pivot). Rows above rank include current pivot row and earlier pivot rows. They may have bit d set. We don't consider them for pivot. If a row below rank has bit d set, we pivot. If not, d free. Good.

Now, basis construction:
```
for f in free_cols:
    vec = 1 << f
    for p in pivot_cols:
        r = pivot_row_for_col[p]
        if (rows[r] >> f) & 1:
            vec |= 1 << p
    basis.append(vec)
```
Need ensure pivot_cols order corresponds to rows and rows[r] is RREF. Yes.

Let's test with a matrix where pivot row has bit in a free column f and also in another pivot column? RREF ensures no other pivot columns. But our elimination eliminates later pivot columns from earlier rows, so yes. Let's verify with sample4: row0 after processing col1 had bits 0,2,3. Pivot cols 0,1. It has no bit1. Good. row1 bits1,2. No bit0. Good.

Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Let's test with a matrix where rank deficient and a free column is also a pivot column? Impossible. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "rank" variable and pivot search range after swapping; we increment rank after setting pivot. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]" when pivot == rank; no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "eliminate this column from all other rows" including rows below that are zero? If bit set, XOR. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "free_cols" includes columns that are pivot? No.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "basis" vectors maybe not independent? They are independent because each has a unique free variable bit set. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "covered" all false if basis empty; all(covered) false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "X" construction if basis vector has bits beyond N? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "rank" variable and pivot search range after swapping; we increment rank after setting pivot. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]" when pivot == rank; no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "eliminate this column from all other rows" including rows below that are zero? If bit set, XOR. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "free_cols" includes columns that are pivot? No.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "basis" vectors maybe not independent? They are independent because each has a unique free variable bit set. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "covered" all false if basis empty; all(covered) false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "X" construction if basis vector has bits beyond N? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "rank" variable and pivot search range after swapping; we increment rank after setting pivot. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]" when pivot == rank; no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "eliminate this column from all other rows" including rows below that are zero? If bit set, XOR. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "free_cols" includes columns that are pivot? No.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "basis" vectors maybe not independent? They are independent because each has a unique free variable bit set. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "covered" all false if basis empty; all(covered) false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "X" construction if basis vector has bits beyond N? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "rank" variable and pivot search range after swapping; we increment rank after setting pivot. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]" when pivot == rank; no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "eliminate this column from all other rows" including rows below that are zero? If bit set, XOR. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "free_cols" includes columns that are pivot? No.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "basis" vectors maybe not independent? They are independent because each has a unique free variable bit set. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "covered" all false if basis empty; all(covered) false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "X" construction if basis vector has bits beyond N? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "rank" variable and pivot search range after swapping; we increment rank after setting pivot. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]" when pivot == rank; no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "eliminate this column from all other rows" including rows below that are zero? If bit set, XOR. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "free_cols" includes columns that are pivot? No.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "basis" vectors maybe not independent? They are independent because each has a unique free variable bit set. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "covered" all false if basis empty; all(covered) false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "X" construction if basis vector has bits beyond N? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "row0=5 bit2=1" after eliminating col1; row0 equation x0 + x2 =0? Actually original row0 x0+x1=0, row1 x1+x2=0. RREF row0 x0+x2=0, row1 x1+x2=0. Basis f=2 gives x0=1,x1=1,x2=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "pivot_cols" order and "pivot_row_for_col" if a pivot row is swapped later? We never swap pivot rows after rank increments? Actually when processing later column, we search from rank; we may swap a row from below into rank. This does not affect earlier pivot rows because rank is after them. So pivot_row_for_col for earlier pivot columns remains correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "rank" variable and pivot search range after swapping; we increment rank after setting pivot. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]" when pivot == rank; no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "eliminate this column from all other rows" including rows below that are zero? If bit set, XOR. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "free_cols" includes columns that are pivot? No.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "basis" vectors maybe not independent? They are independent because each has a unique free variable bit set. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "covered" all false if basis empty; all(covered) false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "X" construction if basis vector has bits beyond N? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? The expression `(rows[r] >> col) & 1` is safe with parentheses. We'll use parentheses. In code, we should write `if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:` with parentheses. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rows[r] >> col & 1" operator precedence. In Python, `>>` has lower precedence than `&`? Matrix A = [[1,1,0],[0,1,1],[0,0,0]]? Not symmetric but test.
Rows: r0=011? bits0,1 =3, r1=110? bits1,2=6, r2=0.
Elimination col0: row0 bit0=1 pivot r0. Eliminate col0 from all: r1 bit0=0, r2=0. pivot_cols[0], rank1.
col1: rows[1]=6 bit1=1 pivot r1. Eliminate col1: row0=3 bit1=1 -> ^=6 =>5 (bits0,2); row2=0. pivot_cols[0,1], rank2.
col2: rows[2]=0 no pivot. free_cols[2].
basis f=2: vec bit2; pivot col0 row0=5 bit2=1 -> set bit0; pivot col1 row1=6 bit2=1 -> set bit1. vec bits0,1,2 =7. Check A*[1,1,1] = row0:1+1=0, row1:1+1=0, row2=0. Yes.
Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
through a final check.

The code computes the nullspace of the adjacency matrix over GF(2), checks whether every vertex appears in at least one nullspace basis vector, and if so, uses those basis vectors as bits of the output integers. This matches the linear-algebra condition.

One thing to verify: if the nullspace basis covers every vertex, using the basis vectors directly as bits gives each vertex a nonzero value. If a vertex is not covered, every valid bit vector has 0 there, so no valid assignment exists. This is correct.

```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, M = map(int, input().split())

    A = [0] * N
    for _ in range(M):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1
        v -= 1
        A[u] ^= 1 << v
        A[v] ^= 1 << u

    rows = A[:]
    pivot_row_for_col = [-1] * N
    pivot_cols = []
    rank = 0

    for col in range(N):
        pivot = -1
        for r in range(rank, N):
            if (rows[r] >> col) & 1:
                pivot = r
                break
        if pivot == -1:
            continue

        rows[rank], rows[pivot] = rows[pivot], rows[rank]

        for r in range(N):
            if r != rank and ((rows[r] >> col) & 1):
                rows[r] ^= rows[rank]

        pivot_row_for_col[col] = rank
        pivot_cols.append(col)
        rank += 1

    free_cols = [c for c in range(N) if pivot_row_for_col[c] == -1]

    basis = []
    for f in free_cols:
        vec = 1 << f
        for p in pivot_cols:
            r = pivot_row_for_col[p]
            if (rows[r] >> f) & 1:
                vec |= 1 << p
        basis.append(vec)

    covered = [False] * N
    for vec in basis:
        x = vec
        while x:
            lsb = x & -x
            v = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            covered[v] = True
            x ^= lsb

    if not all(covered):
        print("No")
        return

    X = [0] * N
    for j, vec in enumerate(basis):
        bit = 1 << j
        x = vec
        while x:
            lsb = x & -x
            v = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            X[v] |= bit
            x ^= lsb

    print("Yes")
    print(" ".join(map(str, X)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
